**Bangladesh**,[^cite_note-15] officially the **People's Republic of Bangladesh**,[^cite_note-16] is a country in [South Asia](21566765.md). It is the [eighth-most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population) in the world with a population of almost 177.8 million people within an area of 148,461 square kilometres (57,321 sq mi).[^cite_note-IMF-9] Bangladesh shares land borders with [India](14533.md) to the north, west, and east, and [Myanmar](19457.md) to the southeast. It has a coastline along the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md) to its south and is separated from [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan) and [Nepal](171166.md) by the [Siliguri Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siliguri_Corridor), and from [China](5405.md) by the [Indian states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_states) of [West Bengal](34040.md) and [Sikkim](140372.md) to its north. [Dhaka](56656.md), the capital and [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Bangladesh), is the nation's political, financial, and cultural centre. [Chittagong](380451.md) is the second-largest city and [the busiest port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Chittagong) of the country.

The territory of modern Bangladesh was ruled by successive [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hindu_empires_and_dynasties) and [Buddhist dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Buddhist_kingdoms_and_empires) in ancient history. Following the [Muslim conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghurid_conquest_of_Bengal) in 1204, the region came under [Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Sultanate) and [Mughal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) rule. As the [largest subdivision of the Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Subah), [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal) became a major commercial centre, known for its [textile industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslin_trade_in_Bengal) and agricultural output. The [Battle of Plassey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Plassey) in 1757 began [British colonial rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Presidency), which lasted nearly two centuries; colonial policies redirected wealth from Bengal to Britain, while the region itself suffered repeated deadly famines. After the [Partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_British_India) in 1947, [East Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Bengal) became the eastern wing of the newly formed [Dominion of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Pakistan) and was later renamed [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan).[^cite_note-Britannica_Bangladesh_history-17]

During more than two decades of political and economic marginalisation by the [West Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Pakistan)–based government, East Pakistan saw growing civil unrest that culminated in the [1948–52 language movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_Language_Movement), [1969 mass uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_East_Pakistan_mass_uprising) and the [1971 non-cooperation movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-cooperation_movement_\(1971\)). Following the Pakistani military's [Operation Searchlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Searchlight), the [Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War) began. The [Mukti Bahini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini), aided by Indian forces, fought a war of independence, during which Pakistani forces carried out the [Bangladesh genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_genocide); Bangladesh became a sovereign nation on 16 December 1971. After independence, [Sheikh Mujibur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman) led the country until his [assassination in 1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman); [Ziaur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziaur_Rahman), who later came to power, was himself [assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Ziaur_Rahman) in 1981. The 1980s were dominated by the dictatorship of [Hussain Muhammad Ershad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussain_Muhammad_Ershad), who was overthrown in a [mass uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Bangladesh_mass_uprising) in 1990. Since the return to democracy in 1991, national politics has largely alternated between the [Awami League](404861.md), led by [Sheikh Hasina](623439.md), and the [Bangladesh Nationalist Party](309760.md), led by [Khaleda Zia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaleda_Zia). After more than 15 years of rule, Hasina was ousted in another [mass uprising](78109234.md) in 2024.[^cite_note-Britannica_Bangladesh_history-17]

Bangladesh is a unitary [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic) based on the [Westminster system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system). It has South Asia's second-largest economy and is one of the largest contributors to [United Nations peacekeeping operations](15652027.md).[^cite_note-UN_peacekeeping_Bangladesh-18] Bangladesh consists of [eight divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divisions_of_Bangladesh), [64 districts](37210460.md), [497 upazilas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upazila), and [4,599 union councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_councils_of_Bangladesh).[^cite_note-Bangladesh_administrative_divisions-19] It is home to the [largest mangrove forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans) in the world and hosts one of the world's largest [refugee populations](53544313.md).[^cite_note-UNHCR_Rohingya_2025-20] Bangladesh continues to face [corruption](38163102.md), [human rights concerns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Bangladesh), political instability, and the [effects of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change). Bangladesh is a member state of [SAARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation) and other regional and international organisations.

## Etymology

Main article: [Names of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Bengal)

The etymology of *Bangladesh* ("Bengali country") can be traced to the early 20th century, when Bengali patriotic songs, such as *Aaji Bangladesher Hridoy* by [Rabindranath Tagore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabindranath_Tagore) and *Namo Namo Namo Bangladesh Momo* by [Kazi Nazrul Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazi_Nazrul_Islam), used the term in 1905 and 1932 respectively.[^cite_note-21] Starting in the 1950s, Bengali nationalists used the term in political rallies in [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan).

The term *Bangla* can refer to both the [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal) region and the [Bengali language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language). The origins of the term *Bangla* are unclear, with theories pointing to a Bronze Age [proto-Dravidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Dravidian_language) tribe,[^cite_note-22] and the Iron Age [Vanga Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanga_Kingdom).[^cite_note-23] The earliest known usage of the term is the [Nesari plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nesari) in 805 AD. The term *Vangala Desa* is found in 11th-century South Indian records.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-Sen-1999-25] The term gained official status during the [Sultanate of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Bengal) in the 14th century.[^cite_note-Ahmed2004-26][^cite_note-27] [Shamsuddin Ilyas Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamsuddin_Ilyas_Shah) proclaimed himself as the first "[Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah) of Bangala" in 1342.[^cite_note-Ahmed2004-26] The word *Bangāl* became the most common name for the region during the Islamic period.[^cite_note-28] Sixteenth century historian [Abu'l-Fazl ibn Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu'l-Fazl_ibn_Mubarak) mentions in his *[Ain-i-Akbari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ain-i-Akbari)* that the addition of the suffix *"al"* came from the fact that the ancient rajahs of the land raised mounds of earth in lowlands at the foot of the hills which were called "al".[^cite_note-29] This is also mentioned in [Ghulam Husain Salim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghulam_Husain_Salim)'s [Riyaz-us-Salatin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riyaz-us-Salatin).[^cite_note-riaj-30]

The Indo-Aryan suffix *[Desh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/-desh)* is derived from the Sanskrit word *deśha*, which means "land" or "country". Hence, the name *Bangladesh* means "Land of Bengal" or "Country of Bengal".[^cite_note-Sen-1999-25]

## History

Main article: [History of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Bangladesh)

### Early history



The [Mahasthan Brahmi Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahasthan_Brahmi_Inscription) from the 3rd century BCE, discovered at [Mahasthangarh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahasthangarh), [Bogra District](2446140.md) in 1931

The first great indigenous empire to cover the territory was the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire) (c. 320–185 BC). Following its decline, the kingdom of [Samatata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samatata) arose, which was a tributary state of the [Gupta Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire) (ca. 319–ca. 540 AD). [Harsha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harsha) (606–47 AD) drew Samatata into its loosely administered political structure. The Buddhist [Pala Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala_Empire) ruled the region from 750 to 1150 AD. It was overthrown by the Hindu [Sena dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sena_dynasty), which ruled the territory until the [Muslim conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_in_the_Indian_subcontinent) led by [Muhammad Bakhtiyar Khalji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Bakhtiyar_Khalji) of the [Ghurid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghurid_dynasty) in 1204.[^cite_note-31]

### Medieval period

Main articles: [Bengal Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Sultanate) and [Bengal Subah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Subah)



A woman in [Dhaka](56656.md) clad in fine [Bengali muslin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslin_trade_in_Bengal), 18th century

Bengal was then incorporated into the [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate) (1206–1526 AD).[^cite_note-islamization-32] In 1341, the independent [Bengal Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Sultanate) was established by [Fakhruddin Mubarak Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fakhruddin_Mubarak_Shah).[^cite_note-islamization-32] Two dynasties ruled the independent Sultanate of Bengal consecutively, the [Hussain Shahi dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussain_Shahi_dynasty) and the [Ilyas Shahi dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilyas_Shahi_dynasty), with a brief reign of the [House of Ganesha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Ganesha). Amid geographic expansion and economic prosperity, it was regarded by European and Chinese visitors as the "richest country to trade with".[^cite_note-33]<sup>: 10 </sup> The [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) conquered Bengal in 1576.[^cite_note-islamization-32][^cite_note-34] By the 18th century, the [Bengal Subah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Subah) emerged as one of the wealthiest provinces of the empire and was described as the "Paradise of Countries" and the "breadbasket of India".[^cite_note-islamization-32] The region was a major global exporter and producer of cotton textiles ([muslin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslin_trade_in_Bengal) in particular), silk and [shipbuilding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipbuilding_in_Bangladesh).[^cite_note-ray-35]<sup>: 174 </sup> Following the decline of the Mughal Empire in the early 1700s, the region became a semi-independent state under the [Nawabs of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawabs_of_Bengal), founded by [Murshid Quli Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murshid_Quli_Khan) in 1717.

### British colonial rule

Main article: [Bengal Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Presidency)



&#x20; Bengal Presidency

&#x20; British India

On 23 June 1757, the state led by [Siraj-ud-Daulah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siraj-ud-Daulah) was defeated by the British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) in the [Battle of Plassey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Plassey)—which helped establish colonial [British rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) over Bengal and the wider Indian subcontinent. Bengal's economy became connected to the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) at the expense of an extraordinary [capital flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_flight) and deindustrialisation following colonial extraction and the collapse of the Bengali textile industry.[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-ray-35]<sup>: 7–10 </sup> Estimates of deaths during the [Great Bengal famine of 1770](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Bengal_famine_of_1770) range from one to ten million,[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38][^cite_note-Irschick2018-39] killing up to one-third of the total population of the Bengal Presidency.[^cite_note-40]<sup>: 47 </sup>

### As a part of Pakistan

Main articles: [Dominion of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Pakistan), [East Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Bengal), and [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan)

Further information: [West Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Pakistan), [Partition of Bengal (1947)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_Bengal_\(1947\)), and [Partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India)

In the aftermath of [direct British rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Presidency) for nearly two centuries, the borders of modern Bangladesh were established with the [partition of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_Bengal_\(1947\)) between India and Pakistan by the [Radcliffe Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radcliffe_Line)[^cite_note-41] during the [partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) on 15 August 1947, when the region became [East Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Bengal) as the eastern and most populous wing of the newly formed [Dominion of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Pakistan)—alongside [West Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Pakistan).[^cite_note-42] The western and eastern wings of the newly formed Pakistan were geographically separated by a distance of over 1,000 miles, which became the root cause of deep [economic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality).[^cite_note-43] Khawaja Nazimuddin was East Bengal's first [chief minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_East_Bengal) with [Frederick Chalmers Bourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Chalmers_Bourne) its governor. The [All Pakistan Awami Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Pakistan_Awami_Muslim_League) was formed in 1949. In 1950, the [East Bengal Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Bengal_Legislative_Assembly) enacted [land reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reform), abolishing the Permanent Settlement and the zamindari system.[^cite_note-44] The Awami Muslim League was renamed as a more "secular" [Awami League](404861.md) in 1953.[^cite_note-LewisSagar1992-45] The first constituent assembly was dissolved in 1954. The [United Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_\(East_Pakistan\)) coalition led by [A. K. Fazlul Huq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._K._Fazlul_Huq) swept aside the Muslim League in a landslide victory in the [1954 East Bengali legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_East_Bengali_legislative_election). East Bengal was renamed [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan) in 1956 as part of the [One Unit Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Unit_Scheme) under the [new constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956_Constitution_of_Pakistan), and the province became a vital part of the [Southeast Asia Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organization).

Amidst rising cultural and societal differences, the brutal government crackdown on the 1952 [Bengali language movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language_movement) to establish [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language) as the official language of Pakistan spurred [Bengali nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_nationalism) and [pro-democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-democracy) movements. Pakistan adopted a new constitution in 1956.[^cite_note-46] The [Pakistan Armed Forces](23369.md) imposed [martial law](50477.md) in 1958, following a [coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1958_Pakistani_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), with [Ayub Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayub_Khan_\(general\)) establishing a dictatorship for over a decade. A new constitution was introduced in 1962, replacing the [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system) with a presidential and gubernatorial system (based on [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_college) selection) known as "Basic Democracy".[^cite_note-47] In 1962, Dhaka became the seat of the [National Assembly of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Pakistan), a move seen as appeasing increased Bengali nationalism.[^cite_note-48] In 1966, Awami League leader [Sheikh Mujibur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman) announced a [six-point movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six_point_movement) for a federal parliamentary democracy.



A student procession at the [University of Dhaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Dhaka) campus during the mass protests and riots of 1969

Ethnic, linguistic, and cultural discrimination was common in Pakistan's civil and military services, in which Bengalis were under-represented;[^cite_note-49] leading to East Pakistan forging a distinct political identity.[^cite_note-50] Authorities banned Bengali literature and music in the state media.[^cite_note-51] The Pakistani government practised extensive [economic discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_discrimination) against East Pakistan, including the refusal for foreign aid allocation.[^cite_note-google.co.nz-52] Despite generating 70% of Pakistan's export revenue with jute and tea, East Pakistan received much less government spending. Notable economists from East Pakistan, including [Rehman Sobhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehman_Sobhan) and [Nurul Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurul_Islam_\(economist\)) demanded a separate foreign exchange account for the eastern wing, also pointing to the existence of two different economies within Pakistan itself, dubbed the Two-Economies Theory.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] The populist leader Sheikh Mujibur Rahman was arrested for treason in the [Agartala Conspiracy Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agartala_Conspiracy_Case) and was released during the [1969 uprising in East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_uprising_in_East_Pakistan) which resulted in Ayub Khan's resignation. General [Yahya Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Khan) assumed power, reintroducing martial law.

After the Pakistan Government's poor response to the [1970 Bhola Cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_Bhola_Cyclone) which left over 500,000 people dead,[^cite_note-countrystudies.us1-55] [Abdul Hamid Khan Bhashani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hamid_Khan_Bhashani) a prominent political figure who had previously led multiple rebellion against the [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) was the first ever Bengali to declare independence of East Pakistan in a massive public rally on 23 November 1970 but it was not officially recognized.[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57] After the December 1970 elections, the Bengali-nationalist Awami League won 167 of 169 East Pakistani seats in the National Assembly. The League claimed the right to form a government and develop a new constitution but was strongly opposed by the Pakistani military and the [Pakistan Peoples Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Peoples_Party) led by [Zulfikar Ali Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulfikar_Ali_Bhutto).

The [7 March Speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_March_Speech_of_Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman) of Mujib led to a [non-cooperation movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-cooperation_movement_\(1971\)). The autocratic Pakistani government then initiated [Operation Searchlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Searchlight) on 25 March 1971 in response.[^cite_note-58] While Mujib was arrested by the Pakistani Army on 26 March 1971, freedom fighter Major [Ziaur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziaur_Rahman) publicly broadcast the independence of Bangladesh on behalf of Mujib.[^cite_note-59] A nine-month-long bloody [liberation war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War) ensued, during which [a genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_genocide) of ethnic Bengalis occurred.[^cite_note-60] The culmination of the war established Bangladesh as a sovereign nation following [Pakistani surrender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender) on 16 December 1971.

### Independent Bangladesh

The [Constitution of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Bangladesh) was enacted on 4 November 1972.[^cite_note-61] Following independence, the Mujib-led government faced allegations of corruption and mismanagement during a period of lawlessness and economic crisis. Efforts to establish [One-party socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAKSAL) and a [large famine in 1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_famine_of_1974) led to [Mujib's assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman) in 1975 following a significant decline in his popularity.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-gov0-63]<sup>: 131 </sup> The presidency was then transferred to [Ziaur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziaur_Rahman), who re-established public order, industrialised agriculture, founded the [Bangladesh Nationalist Party](309760.md) and initiated the creation of the [South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation).[^cite_note-64] Ziaur Rahman founded the Bangladesh Nationalist Party on 1 September 1978.[^cite_note-65] Following [Rahman's assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Ziaur_Rahman) in 1981, the ensuing decade was a [military dictatorship](146728.md) under [Hussain Muhammad Ershad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussain_Muhammad_Ershad) that saw infrastructural development, [devolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) reforms, privatisation of nationalised industries and the declaration of Islam as the [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion) in 1988.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]



Victory march by protesters after the [resignation of Sheikh Hasina](77539154.md) in 2024

After the [restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Bangladesh_mass_uprising) of [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy) in [1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Bangladeshi_general_election), power alternated between [Khaleda Zia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaleda_Zia) of the BNP and [Sheikh Hasina](623439.md) of the Awami League, an era dubbed the [Battle of Begums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Begums)—which shaped national politics until 2024.[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-gov0-63]<sup>: 130 </sup> The country under Sheikh Hasina's leadership saw economic progress alongside criticism over [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding), [corruption](38163102.md), and [human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Bangladesh).[^cite_note-71] Hasina won her second, third and fourth consecutive terms in the [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Bangladeshi_general_election), [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Bangladeshi_general_election) and the [2024 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Bangladeshi_general_election); the 2014, 2018 and 2024 elections were criticized by opposition parties and outside observers over boycotts, violence, and restrictions on political competition.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] Following a [July Uprising](78109234.md) against the authoritarian government, Hasina was [forced to resign and flee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-cooperation_movement_\(2024\)) to India on [5 August 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_36).[^cite_note-75] An interim government was formed in August 2024, with Nobel laureate [Muhammad Yunus](548105.md) as the [Chief Adviser](9219750.md).[^cite_note-76] After the [2026 general election](72614737.md) the [Bangladesh Nationalist Party](309760.md) (BNP) returned to power with Chairman [Tarique Rahman](5601422.md) being inaugurated as the Prime Minister of Bangladesh on 17 February 2026. [Bangladesh Jamaat-e-Islami](21739288.md) became the main opposition party.[^cite_note-77]

Since the early 1990s, driven by [free market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market) policies and [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation) measures, Bangladesh's economy has grown rapidly, driven in part by its large [textile industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry_in_Bangladesh), which is the second-largest in the world.[^cite_note-78] It has emerged as the [second-largest economy in South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)), surpassing the nominal GDP per capita of neighboring India since 2018.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] Bangladesh has reduced its [poverty rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Bangladesh), which has gone down from 80% in 1971,[^cite_note-81] to 44.2% in 1991,[^cite_note-82] and all the way down to 18.7% in 2022.[^cite_note-83] Its [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index) growth during the 21st century was surpassed only by China.[^cite_note-84] As part of the [green transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_transition), Bangladesh's industrial sector emerged as a leader in building [green factories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_factory), with the country having the largest number of certified green factories in the world.[^cite_note-85] It has also given shelter to [over a million Rohingya refugees](53544313.md) fleeing the [Rohingya genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_genocide) since 2017, which has strained its resources and highlighted its humanitarian commitments.[^cite_note-86]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Bangladesh)



Physical map of Bangladesh

Bangladesh is in South Asia on the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md). It is surrounded almost entirely by neighbouring India, and shares a small border with [Myanmar](19457.md) to its southeast, though it lies very close to [Nepal](171166.md), [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), and China. The country is divided into three regions. Most of the country is dominated by the fertile [Ganges Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_Delta), the largest river delta in the world.[^cite_note-87] The northwest and central parts of the country are formed by the [Madhupur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhupur_tract) and the [Barind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barind_Tract) plateaus. The northeast and southeast are home to [evergreen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evergreen) hill ranges.

The Ganges delta is formed by the confluence of the Ganges (local name [Padma](1538264.md) or *Pôdda*), [Brahmaputra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmaputra_River) ([Jamuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamuna_River_\(Bangladesh\)) or *Jomuna*), and [Meghna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meghna_River) rivers and their tributaries. The Ganges unites with the Jamuna (main channel of the Brahmaputra) and later joins the Meghna, finally flowing into the Bay of Bengal. Bangladesh is called the "Land of Rivers",[^cite_note-88] as it is home to over 57 [trans-boundary rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-boundary_river), the most of any nation-state. Water issues are politically complicated since Bangladesh is downstream of India.[^cite_note-89]

Bangladesh is predominantly rich fertile flat land. Most of it is less than 12 m (39 ft) above sea level, and it is estimated that about 10% of its land would be flooded if the sea level were to rise by 1 m (3.3 ft).[^cite_note-ali-90] 12% of the country is covered by hill systems. The country's [haor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haor) wetlands are of significance to global environmental science. The [highest point in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mountains_of_Bangladesh) is the [Saka Haphong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka_Haphong), located near the border with Myanmar, with an elevation of 1,064 m (3,491 ft).[^cite_note-cia-91] Previously, either [Keokradong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keokradong) or [Tazing Dong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tazing_Dong) were considered the highest.

In Bangladesh [forest cover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_cover) is around 14% of the total land area, equivalent to 1,883,400 hectares (ha) of forest in 2020, down from 1,920,330 hectares (ha) in 1990. In 2020, naturally regenerating forest covered 1,725,330 hectares (ha) and planted forest covered 158,070 hectares (ha). Of the naturally regenerating forest 0% was reported to be [primary forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_forest) (consisting of native tree species with no clearly visible indications of human activity) and around 33% of the forest area was found within protected areas. For the year 2015, 100% of the forest area was reported to be under [public ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_ownership).[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93]

### Climate

Main article: [Geography of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Bangladesh)



A photo from space showing the pollution over Bangladesh

Straddling the [Tropic of Cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Cancer), Bangladesh's climate is tropical, with a mild winter from October to March and a hot, humid summer from March to June. The country has never recorded an air temperature below 0 °C (32 °F), with a record low of 1.1 °C (34.0 °F) in the northwest city of [Dinajpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinajpur) on 3 February 1905.[^cite_note-94] A warm and humid [monsoon](57630.md) season lasts from June to October and supplies most of the country's rainfall. Natural calamities, such as [floods](4513800.md), [tropical cyclones](8282374.md), [tornadoes](37530.md), and [tidal bores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_bore) occur almost every year,[^cite_note-NatDis-95] combined with the effects of [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation), [soil degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soils_retrogression_and_degradation) and [erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erosion). The [cyclones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Bangladesh_tropical_cyclones) of 1970 and 1991 were particularly devastating, the [latter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Bangladesh_cyclone) killing approximately 140,000 people.[^cite_note-96]

&#x20;In September 1998, Bangladesh saw the [most severe flooding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Bangladesh_floods) in modern history, after which two-thirds of the country went underwater, along with a death toll of 1,000.[^cite_note-EWG-97] As a result of various international and national level initiatives in disaster risk reduction, the human toll and economic damage from floods and cyclones have come down over the years.[^cite_note-98] The [2007 South Asian floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_South_Asian_floods) ravaged areas across the country, leaving five million people displaced, with a death toll around 500.[^cite_note-99]&#x20;



Flooding after the [1991 Bangladesh cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Bangladesh_cyclone), which killed around 140,000 people

#### Climate change

Main article: [Climate change in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Bangladesh)

Bangladesh is recognised to be one of the countries most [vulnerable to climate change](66204363.md).[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101] Over the course of a century, 508 cyclones have affected the Bay of Bengal region, 17% of which are believed to have made landfall in Bangladesh.[^cite_note-102] [Natural hazards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_hazards) that come from increased rainfall, rising sea levels, and tropical cyclones are expected to increase as the climate changes, each seriously affecting agriculture, water and food security, human health, and shelter.[^cite_note-103] It is estimated that by 2050, a three-foot rise in sea levels will inundate some 20% of the land and displace more than 30 million people.[^cite_note-104] To address the [sea level rise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level_rise) threat in Bangladesh, the [Bangladesh Delta Plan 2100](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Delta_Plan_2100) has been launched.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106]

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Fauna of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Bangladesh)



A [Bengal tiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_tiger), the national animal, in the Sundarbans

Bangladesh is located in the [Indomalayan realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indomalayan_realm), and lies within four terrestrial ecoregions: [Lower Gangetic Plains moist deciduous forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Gangetic_Plains_moist_deciduous_forests), [Mizoram–Manipur–Kachin rain forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizoram%E2%80%93Manipur%E2%80%93Kachin_rain_forests), [Sundarbans freshwater swamp forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans_freshwater_swamp_forests), and [Sundarbans mangroves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans_mangroves).[^cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-107] Its ecology includes a long sea coastline, numerous [rivers and tributaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_in_Bangladesh), lakes, [wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetland), evergreen forests, semi evergreen forests, hill forests, moist [deciduous forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deciduous_forest), freshwater swamp forests and flat land with tall grass. The Bangladesh Plain is famous for its fertile [alluvial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alluvial) soil which supports extensive cultivation. The country is dominated by lush vegetation, with villages often buried in groves of [mango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mango), [jackfruit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackfruit), [bamboo](39029.md), [betel nut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betel_nut), [coconut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut), and [date palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_palm).[^cite_note-global.britannica.com-108] The country has up to 6000 species of plant life, including 5000 flowering plants.[^cite_note-109] Water bodies and wetland systems provide a habitat for many aquatic plants. [Water lilies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymphaeaceae) and [lotuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelumbo_nucifera) grow vividly during the monsoon season. The country has [60 wildlife sanctuaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Bangladesh).[^cite_note-110]

Bangladesh is home to most of the [Sundarbans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans), the world's largest [mangrove forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove_forest), covering an area of 6,000 square kilometres (2,300 sq mi) in the southwest littoral region. It is divided into three protected sanctuaries: the [South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans_South_Wildlife_Sanctuary), [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans_East_Wildlife_Sanctuary), and [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans_West_Wildlife_Sanctuary) zones. The forest is a UNESCO World Heritage Site.[^cite_note-111] The northeastern Sylhet region is home to haor wetlands, a distinctive ecosystem. It also includes [tropical and subtropical coniferous forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_coniferous_forests), a [freshwater swamp forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freshwater_swamp_forest), and mixed deciduous forests. The southeastern Chittagong region covers evergreen and semi-evergreen hilly jungles. Central Bangladesh includes the plainland Sal forest running along with the districts of Gazipur, [Tangail](1997520.md), and [Mymensingh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mymensingh).[^cite_note-112] [St. Martin's Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Martin's_Island) is the only [coral reef](87410.md) in the country.[^cite_note-113]

Bangladesh has an abundance of [wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_in_Bangladesh) in its forests, marshes, woodlands, and hills.[^cite_note-global.britannica.com-108] The vast majority of animals dwell within a habitat of 150,000 square kilometres (58,000 sq mi).[^cite_note-114] The [Bengal tiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_tiger), [clouded leopard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clouded_leopard), [saltwater crocodile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saltwater_crocodile), [black panther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_panther) and [fishing cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing_cat) are among the chief predators in the Sundarbans.[^cite_note-115] Northern and eastern Bangladesh is home to the [Asian elephant](379035.md), [hoolock gibbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoolock_gibbon), [Asian black bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_black_bear) and [oriental pied hornbill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_pied_hornbill).[^cite_note-bearprojectbd.weebly.com-116] The [chital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chital) deer are widely seen in southwestern woodlands. Other animals include the [black giant squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_giant_squirrel), [capped langur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capped_langur), [Bengal fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_fox), [sambar deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambar_deer), [jungle cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jungle_cat), [king cobra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_cobra), [wild boar](52255.md), [mongooses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongoose), [pangolins](51124.md), [pythons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_\(genus\)) and [water monitors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_water_monitor). Bangladesh has one of the largest populations of [Irrawaddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrawaddy_dolphins) and [Ganges dolphins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_river_dolphin).[^cite_note-117] The country has numerous species of [amphibians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibian) (53), reptiles (139), [marine reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_reptile) (19) and [marine mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_mammal) (5). It also has [628 species of birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_birds_of_Bangladesh).[^cite_note-118]

Several animals became extinct in Bangladesh during the last century, including the one-horned and two-horned [rhinoceros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhinoceros) and common [peafowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peafowl). The human population is concentrated in urban areas, limiting deforestation to a certain extent. Rapid urban growth has threatened natural habitats. The country has widespread environmental issues; pollution of the [Dhaleshwari River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaleshwari_River) by the textile industry and shrimp cultivation in Chakaria Sundarbans have both been described by academics as [ecocides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecocide).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] Although many areas are protected under law, some Bangladeshi wildlife is threatened by this growth. The [Bangladesh Environment Conservation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Environment_Conservation_Act) was enacted in 1995. The government has designated several regions as [Ecologically Critical Areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecologically_Critical_Area), including wetlands, forests, and rivers. The [Sundarbans tiger project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans_tiger_project) and the Bangladesh Bear Project are among the key initiatives to strengthen conservation.[^cite_note-bearprojectbd.weebly.com-116] It ratified the Rio [Convention on Biological Diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_Biological_Diversity) on 3 May 1994.[^cite_note-cbd.int-121] As of 2014, the country was set to revise its [National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_action_plan).[^cite_note-cbd.int-121]

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Government of Bangladesh](18420484.md), [Politics of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Bangladesh), and [List of People's Republic of Bangladesh governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_People's_Republic_of_Bangladesh_governments)



[Mirza Fakhrul Islam Alamgir](35277638.md)\
[President](404877.md)



[Tarique Rahman](5601422.md)\
[Prime Minister](461887.md)



The [National Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jatiya_Sangsad_Bhaban) building in [Sher-e-Bangla Nagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sher-e-Bangla_Nagar), a neighbourhood named after the first [Prime Minister of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Bengal)



[Bangabhaban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangabhaban) (Translation: *Bengal House*) is the [presidential palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_palace) of Bangladesh. It was originally a house for the [Viceroy of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroy_of_India) and the [Governor of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Bengal_Presidency).

Bangladesh, by [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Bangladesh), is a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state)[^cite_note-gov2-122] and a *[de jure](43594.md)* [representative democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_democracy) with a [Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system)-style [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic)[^cite_note-gov0-63] that has [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage).[^cite_note-gov-123] The [government](18420484.md) can be divided into three pillars: the executive, the legislative and the [judiciary](50847537.md). All function to ensure accountability, transparency and [checks and balances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers).[^cite_note-gov2-122] Since its independence, the [Awami League](404861.md) (AL) and the [Bangladesh Nationalist Party](309760.md) (BNP) have remained two of the most powerful [political parties in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Bangladesh).[^cite_note-gov0-63]

- The first pillar of the government is the executive organ, which is entrusted with the administration of the country.

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;Executive powers are largely vested in the [Prime Minister](461887.md), who is the [head of government](37570.md) and oversees the [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Bangladesh).

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;The tenure of a parliamentary government is five years. Various ministers form the bulk of the executive organ, overseeing government departments and forming policies.

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;The [Civil Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Civil_Service) assists the ministers in implementing policy.

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;All authorities unite to formulate policies, manage public services, and implement national development plans.

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;The [President](404877.md) is the ceremonial [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state), whose powers include signing bills passed by parliament into law, maintaining the government's stability and continuity,

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;and fulfilling their duties as the commander-in-chief of the [Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Armed_Forces)

  [^cite_note-124]

  &#x20;and the [chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor) of all universities.

  [^cite_note-125]

- The second pillar of the government is the legislative organ, also known as the [Jatiya Sangsad](577333.md) (House of the Nation).

  [^cite_note-parliament-126]

  &#x20;Citizens elect the members of parliament (MPs).

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;The [unicameral](204304.md) parliament has 350 MPs, including 300 elected on the [first past the post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_past_the_post) system and 50 appointed to reserved seats for [women's empowerment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_empowerment).

  [^cite_note-parliament-126]

  [^cite_note-127]

  &#x20;[Article 70 of the Constitution of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_70_of_the_Constitution_of_Bangladesh) forbids MPs from voting against their party.

  [^cite_note-128]

  &#x20;The parliament is presided over by the [Speaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Jatiya_Sangsad), who is second in line to the president.

  [^cite_note-129]

- The third pillar of the government is the judiciary organ, which is in charge of interpreting the law, resolving conflicts, and maintaining justice.

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;The [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Bangladesh) is the highest court, separated into the [Appellate Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appellate_Division,_Supreme_Court_of_Bangladesh) and the [High Court Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Court_Division,_Supreme_Court_of_Bangladesh).

  [^cite_note-court-130]

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;It is led by the [Chief Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Bangladesh).

  [^cite_note-court-130]

  &#x20;The judiciary has the power to assess a law's constitutionality and offer legal remedies. It protects citizens' rights, ensures the law is applied fairly, and preserves the balance of power within the government.

  [^cite_note-gov2-122]

  &#x20;The courts have wide latitude in [judicial review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_review_in_Bangladesh), and judicial [precedent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precedent) is supported by Article 111 of the constitution.

  [^cite_note-131]

  &#x20;The judiciary includes district and metropolitan courts divided into civil and criminal courts. Due to a shortage of judges, the judiciary has a large backlog.

  [^cite_note-132]

According to [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance)'s Global State of Democracy (GSoD) Indices and Democracy Tracker, Bangladesh performs in the low range on overall democratic measures, with particular weaknesses in political representation, including credible elections, inclusive suffrage and elected government.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative geography of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_geography_of_Bangladesh)

Further information: [Divisions of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divisions_of_Bangladesh), [Districts of Bangladesh](37210460.md), and [Upazilas of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upazilas_of_Bangladesh)

A clickable map of Bangladesh exhibiting its divisions.

A clickable map of Bangladesh exhibiting its divisions.

Bangladesh is divided into eight administrative divisions,[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-cia-91][^cite_note-137] each named after its headquarters: [Barisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barisal_Division) (officially *Barishal*[^cite_note-namechange-138]), [Chittagong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chittagong_Division) (officially *Chattogram*[^cite_note-namechange-138]), [Dhaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaka_Division), [Khulna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khulna_Division), [Mymensingh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mymensingh_Division), [Rajshahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajshahi_Division), [Rangpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangpur_Division), and [Sylhet](577594.md).

Divisions are subdivided into districts (*zila*). Bangladesh has 64 districts, each subdivided into *upazila* (subdistricts) or *thana*. The area within each police station, except for those in metropolitan areas, is divided into *[unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Councils_of_Bangladesh)*, with each union comprising multiple villages. In the metropolitan areas, police stations are divided into wards, further divided into *mahallas*.[^cite_note-Bangladesh_administrative_divisions-19]

There are no elected officials at the divisional or district levels, and the administration comprises only government officials. Direct elections are held in each union (or ward) for a chairperson and several members. In 1997, Parliament passed legislation reserving three seats (out of 12) in every union for women.[^cite_note-139]

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Bangladesh)



Countries that share diplomatic relations with Bangladesh

Bangladesh is considered a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) in [global politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_politics).[^cite_note-142] It plays an important role in the geopolitical affairs of the [Indo-Pacific](330456.md),[^cite_note-143] due to its strategic location between South and Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-144] Bangladesh joined the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) in 1972 and the United Nations in 1974.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146] It relies on multilateral diplomacy on issues like climate change, nuclear non-proliferation, trade policy and non-traditional security issues.[^cite_note-147] Bangladesh pioneered the creation of [SAARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation), the preeminent forum for regional diplomacy on the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent).[^cite_note-148] It joined the [OIC](47725.md) in 1974,[^cite_note-149] and is a founding member of the [Developing-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D-8_Organization_for_Economic_Cooperation).[^cite_note-150] Recently, Bangladesh has focused on promoting regional trade and transport links with support from the [World Bank](36752.md).[^cite_note-151] It is also [attempting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_of_Bangladesh_to_ASEAN) to join [ASEAN](106539.md) as one of its top foreign policy goals.[^cite_note-\:5-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] Dhaka hosts the headquarters of [BIMSTEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BIMSTEC), an organisation that brings together [countries dependent on the Bay of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_dependent_on_the_Bay_of_Bengal).

[Relations](27812760.md) with neighbouring Myanmar have been severely strained since 2016–2017, after over 700,000 [Rohingya refugees](53544313.md) fled into Bangladesh.[^cite_note-RohingyaHRW-155] Bangladesh's parliament, government, and civil society have been at the forefront of [international criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reaction_to_the_2016%E2%80%9317_Rohingya_exodus) against Myanmar for military operations against the Rohingya, and have demanded their [right of return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_return) to [Arakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arakan).[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157]

Bangladesh shares an important [bilateral and economic relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh%E2%80%93India_relations) with its largest neighbour, India,[^cite_note-158] which is often strained by [water politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_politics_in_India) of the [Ganges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_water_dispute) and the [Teesta](1365385.md#Water_sharing_challenge),[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] and the [border killings of Bangladeshi civilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deaths_along_the_Bangladesh%E2%80%93India_border).[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163] Bangladesh's relationship with Pakistan is problematic, mainly because Pakistan denies the 1971 [Bangladesh genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_genocide).[^cite_note-164] It maintains a [warm relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh-China_relations) with China, its largest trading partner and largest arms supplier.[^cite_note-165] Japan is Bangladesh's largest economic aid provider, and the two have a [strategic and economic partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh%E2%80%93Japan_relations).[^cite_note-166] Political relations with Middle Eastern countries are robust.[^cite_note-167] Bangladesh receives 59% of its remittances from the Middle East,[^cite_note-168] despite poor working conditions affecting over four million [Bangladeshi workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladeshis_in_the_Middle_East).[^cite_note-169] Bangladesh plays a major role in [global climate](5042951.md) diplomacy as a leader of the [Climate Vulnerable Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Vulnerable_Forum).[^cite_note-170]

### Military

Main article: [Bangladesh Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Armed_Forces)



Bangladesh Army personnel on a [BTR-80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BTR-80)



Bangladesh Navy [Frigate](11464.md), [BNS Khalid Bin Walid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNS_Khalid_Bin_Walid)



Mig-29 and F-7, Fighter aircraft's of Bangladesh Air Force

The [Bangladesh Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Armed_Forces) have inherited the institutional framework of the [British military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_military) and the [British Indian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Indian_Army).[^cite_note-171] In 2024, the active personnel strength of the Bangladesh Armed Forces was around 230,000,[^cite_note-IISS-172] including the Air Force (21,000) and the Navy (27,000).[^cite_note-173] In addition to traditional defence roles, the military has supported civil authorities in disaster relief and provided internal security during periods of political unrest. For many years, Bangladesh has been the world's largest contributor to [UN peacekeeping forces](15652027.md).[^cite_note-174] The [military budget of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_budget_of_Bangladesh) accounts for 1.3% of GDP, amounting to US\$4.3 billion in 2021.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176]

The [Bangladesh Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Navy), one of the largest in the Bay of Bengal, includes a [fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_active_ships_of_the_Bangladesh_Navy) of frigates, submarines, corvettes, and other vessels. The [Bangladesh Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Air_Force) has a [small fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_active_Bangladesh_military_aircraft) of multi-role combat aircraft. Most of Bangladesh's military equipment comes from China.[^cite_note-177] In recent years, Bangladesh and India have increased joint military exercises, high-level visits of military leaders, [counter-terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-terrorism) cooperation and intelligence sharing. Bangladesh is vital to ensuring stability and security in [northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India).[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179]

Bangladesh's strategic importance in the eastern subcontinent hinges on its proximity to China, its frontier with Burma, the separation of mainland and northeast India, and its maritime territory in the Bay of Bengal.[^cite_note-180] In 2002, Bangladesh and China signed a Defense Cooperation Agreement.[^cite_note-181] The United States has pursued negotiations with Bangladesh on a [Status of forces agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_of_forces_agreement), an [Acquisition and Cross-Servicing Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acquisition_and_Cross-Servicing_Agreement) and a General Security of Military Information Agreement.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] In 2019, Bangladesh ratified the UN [Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons).[^cite_note-185]

### Civil society

Main article: [List of non-governmental organisations in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_non-governmental_organisations_in_Bangladesh)

Since the colonial period, Bangladesh has had a prominent [civil society](209443.md). There are various special interest groups, including [non-governmental organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-governmental_organisations), human rights organisations, professional associations, [chambers of commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_commerce), employers' associations, and trade unions.[^cite_note-186] The [National Human Rights Commission of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Human_Rights_Commission_of_Bangladesh) was set up in 2007. Notable human rights organisations and initiatives include the [Centre for Law and Mediation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_Law_and_Mediation_\(Bangladesh\)), [Odhikar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odhikar), the [Alliance for Bangladesh Worker Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_for_Bangladesh_Worker_Safety), the [Bangladesh Environmental Lawyers Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Environmental_Lawyers_Association), the [Bangladesh Hindu Buddhist Christian Unity Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Hindu_Buddhist_Christian_Unity_Council) and the [War Crimes Fact Finding Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Crimes_Fact_Finding_Committee). The world's largest international NGO [BRAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRAC_\(organisation\)) is based in Bangladesh. There has been concern about the shrinking space for independent civil society in recent years.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Bangladesh)

Armed men in black uniforms on a street

The [Rapid Action Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Action_Battalion) (RAB) have been widely accused of [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing), [enforced disappearances](686148.md) and human right abuses. The [United States Department of the Treasury](53667.md) sanctioned RAB in 2021.

[^cite_note-190]

[Torture](47702.md) is banned by the [Constitution of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Bangladesh),[^cite_note-191] but is rampantly used by Bangladesh's security forces. Bangladesh joined the [Convention against Torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_against_Torture) in 1998 and enacted its first anti-torture law, the [Torture and Custodial Death (Prevention) Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture_and_Custodial_Death_\(Prevention\)_Act), in 2013. The first conviction under this law was announced in 2020.[^cite_note-192] Amnesty International [Prisoners of Conscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoner_of_conscience) from Bangladesh have included [Saber Hossain Chowdhury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saber_Hossain_Chowdhury) and [Shahidul Alam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahidul_Alam).[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] The widely criticised [Digital Security Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_Security_Act) was repealed and replaced by the Cyber Security Act in 2023.[^cite_note-195] The repeal was welcomed by the [International Press Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Press_Institute).[^cite_note-196]

On [International Human Rights Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Human_Rights_Day) in December 2021, the [United States Department of the Treasury](53667.md) announced [sanctions](411315.md) on commanders of the [Rapid Action Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Action_Battalion) for extrajudicial killings, torture, and other human rights abuses.[^cite_note-197] [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) has criticised the government for human rights abuses, the crackdown on the opposition, mass media, and civil society through politicised enforcement.[^cite_note-Freedom_House_Bangladesh_profile-198] Bangladesh is ranked "partly free" in Freedom House's *[Freedom in the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World)* report,[^cite_note-Freedom_House_Bangladesh_profile-198] but its [press freedom](105323.md) has deteriorated from "free" to "not free" in recent years due to increasing government pressure.[^cite_note-199] According to the British [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md), the country has a [hybrid regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_regime): the third of four rankings in its [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Index).[^cite_note-200] Bangladesh ranked 96th among 163 countries in the 2022 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index).[^cite_note-201] According to National Human Rights Commission, 70% of alleged human-rights violations are committed by law-enforcement agencies.[^cite_note-202]

[LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Bangladesh) are frowned upon among social conservatives.[^cite_note-203] Homosexuality is affected by [Section 377](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_377) of the [Penal Code of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_Code_of_Bangladesh), originally enacted by the British colonial government.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205] The government only recognises the [transgender](19904525.md) and [intersex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersex) community known as the [Hijra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijra_\(South_Asia\)).[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] According to the 2023 Global Slavery Index, an estimated 1.2 million people were enslaved in Bangladesh as of 2021, which is among the highest in the world.[^cite_note-208]

### Corruption

Main article: [Corruption in Bangladesh](38163102.md)

Like many developing countries, [institutional corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_corruption) is an issue of concern for Bangladesh. Bangladesh ranked 146th among 180 countries on [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International)'s 2018 [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index).[^cite_note-209] Land administration was the sector with the most bribery in 2015,[^cite_note-210] followed by education,[^cite_note-211] police[^cite_note-212] and water supply.[^cite_note-213] The [Anti Corruption Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti_Corruption_Commission_Bangladesh) was formed in 2004, and was active during the [2006–2008 Bangladeshi political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006%E2%80%932008_Bangladeshi_political_crisis), indicting many leading politicians, bureaucrats and businessmen for [graft](2717335.md).[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Bangladesh)

Further information: [List of companies of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_of_Bangladesh)



[Motijheel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motijheel_Thana) in the nation's capital Dhaka is the largest [commercial district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_business_district) in the city.



The [Padma Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padma_Bridge), opened in 2022, is a [road-rail bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_road%E2%80%93rail_bridges) which spans the [Padma River](1538264.md).

Bangladesh is a [lower-middle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GNI_\(nominal\)_per_capita#Lower-middle-income_group) income [mixed-market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy).[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218] It has the world's [36th-largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) by nominal terms, and the [24th-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) by [PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity). Bangladesh has a [labour force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_force) of 71.4 million,[^cite_note-219] which is the world's [seventh-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_labour_force), with an unemployment rate of 3.6% as of 2024.[^cite_note-220] Its [foreign exchange reserves](1015869.md), although depleting,[^cite_note-221] remain the [second-highest in South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign_exchange_reserves), after India. Bangladesh's [large diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladeshi_diaspora) contributed roughly \$27 billion in [remittances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remittance_to_Bangladesh) in 2024.[^cite_note-222] The [Bangladeshi taka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladeshi_taka) is the national currency.

As of 2023, the large service sector accounts for about 51.5% of total GDP, followed by the industrial sector (34.6%), while the [agriculture sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Bangladesh) is by far the smallest, making up only 11% of total GDP.[^cite_note-CIA-2021-8] Although it is the largest employment sector, it provides roughly half of the total workforce.[^cite_note-223] Over 84% of the export earnings come from the [textile industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry_in_Bangladesh).[^cite_note-224] Bangladesh is the second-leading garments exporter in the world, and supplies the global [fast fashion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_fashion) industry, exporting to various Western fashion brands.[^cite_note-225] It is also a major producer of [jute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jute_industry_of_Bangladesh),[^cite_note-226] [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_production_in_Bangladesh),[^cite_note-227] [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_fishes_in_Bangladesh),[^cite_note-228] [tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_production_in_Bangladesh),[^cite_note-229] and flowers.[^cite_note-230] Other major industries include [shipbuilding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipbuilding_in_Bangladesh), [pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_industry_in_Bangladesh), [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_industry_in_Bangladesh), [electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics_industry_in_Bangladesh) and [leather goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leather_industry_in_Bangladesh).[^cite_note-231] China is the [largest trading partner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_trading_partners_of_Bangladesh) of Bangladesh, accounting for 15% of the total trade, followed by India, which accounts for 8% of the total trade.[^cite_note-232]

The private sector accounts for 80% of GDP compared to the dwindling role of state-owned companies.[^cite_note-233] Bangladesh's economy is dominated by family-owned [conglomerates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_of_Bangladesh) and small and medium-sized businesses. Some of the largest publicly traded companies in Bangladesh include [BEXIMCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BEXIMCO), [BRAC Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRAC_Bank), [BSRM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BSRM), [GPH Ispat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GPH_Ispat), [Grameenphone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grameenphone), [Summit Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summit_Group), and [Square Pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_Pharmaceuticals).[^cite_note-234] The [Dhaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaka_Stock_Exchange) and [Chittagong Stock Exchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chittagong_Stock_Exchange) are the country's twin [capital markets](41914.md).[^cite_note-235] Its [telecommunications industry](68929.md) is one of the world's fastest growing, with 188.78 million cellphone subscribers at the end of November 2024.[^cite_note-236] Political instability,[^cite_note-237] high inflation, [endemic corruption](38163102.md), insufficient power supplies, and slow implementation of reforms are major challenges to economic growth.

Bangladesh was ranked 106th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2024 and in 2025.[^cite_note-238]

### Energy

Main article: [Electricity sector in Bangladesh](23797261.md)

Further information: [Energy policy of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Bangladesh), [Bangladesh Power Development Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Power_Development_Board), [Natural gas and petroleum in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_and_petroleum_in_Bangladesh), and [Petrobangla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobangla)



The under-construction [Rooppur Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rooppur_Nuclear_Power_Plant) at night



Wind turbines on [Kutubdia Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutubdia_Island)

Bangladesh, a country experiencing daily blackouts several times a day in 2009, achieved 100% electrification by 2022.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] It is gradually transitioning to a [green economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_economy) and has the largest off-grid solar power programme in the world, benefiting 20 million people.[^cite_note-241] An [electric car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_car) called the *Palki* is being developed for production in the country.[^cite_note-242] [Biogas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogas) is being used to produce organic fertiliser.[^cite_note-243] The under-construction [Rooppur Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rooppur_Nuclear_Power_Plant), under-construction with assistance from the Russian company [Rosatom](1641369.md), will be the first operational [nuclear power plant](207347.md) in the country. Its first unit, out of the two total units, is expected to go into operation in 2025.[^cite_note-244]

Bangladesh continues to have huge untapped reserves of natural gas, particularly in its maritime territory.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246] A lack of exploration and decreasing proven reserves have forced Bangladesh to import [LNG](832128.md) from abroad.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] Gas shortages were further exacerbated by the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md).[^cite_note-250] Bangladesh stopped buying [spot price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spot_price) LNG temporarily in July 2022, despite constant load-shedding, due to a steep price hike in the global market.[^cite_note-251] It restarted buying spot price LNG once again in February 2023 as prices eased.[^cite_note-252]

While [government-owned companies in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_government-owned_companies_of_Bangladesh) generate nearly half of Bangladesh's electricity, privately owned companies like the Summit Group and [Orion Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_Group_\(Bangladesh\)) are playing an increasingly important role in both generating electricity, and supplying machinery, reactors, and equipment.[^cite_note-253] Bangladesh increased electricity production capacity from 5 gigawatts in 2009 to 25.5 gigawatts in 2022. It plans to further increase it to 50 [gigawatts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gigawatts) by 2041. US companies like [Chevron](284749.md) and [General Electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric) supply around 55% of Bangladesh's domestic natural gas production and are among the largest investors in power projects. 80% of Bangladesh's installed gas-fired power generation capacity comes from turbines manufactured in the United States.[^cite_note-254]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Bangladesh)



The [Sundarbans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans) is the largest [mangrove forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove_forest) in the world.

The [tourism industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Bangladesh) is expanding, contributing some 3.02% of total GDP.[^cite_note-255] Bangladesh's international tourism receipts in 2019 amounted to \$391 million.[^cite_note-256] The country has [three UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Bangladesh) ([the Mosque City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_Mosque_City_of_Bagerhat), [the Buddhist Vihara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruins_of_the_Buddhist_Vihara_at_Paharpur) and the [Sundarbans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans)) and seven [tentative-list](44940.md#Nominating_process) sites.[^cite_note-257] The [World Travel and Tourism Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Travel_and_Tourism_Council) (WTTC) reported in 2019 that the travel and tourism industry in Bangladesh directly generated 1,180,500 jobs in 2018 or 1.9% of the country's total employment.[^cite_note-2019_report-258] According to the same report, Bangladesh experiences around 125,000 international tourist arrivals per year.[^cite_note-2019_report-258] Domestic spending generated 97.7% of direct travel and tourism gross domestic product (GDP) in 2012.[^cite_note-2013_report-259]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Bangladesh)

Further information: [Bengalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengalis)

| Year | Pop. | ±% p.a. |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1960 | 51,828,660 | — |
| 1971 | 70,276,758 | +2.81% |
| 1980 | 88,016,432 | +2.53% |
| 1990 | 111,633,717 | +2.41% |
| 2000 | 134,544,304 | +1.88% |
| 2010 | 152,201,981 | +1.24% |
| 2023 | 171,466,990 | +0.92% |
| Source: World Bank[^cite_note-unpop-260] |  |  |

Bangladesh had a recorded population of 169.8 million in the [2022 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Bangladeshi_census),[^cite_note-populationcensus2022-10] which rose to 171.4 million as of 2023.[^cite_note-unpop-260] It is the [eighth-most-populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_population) in the world, the [fifth-most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_population) in Asia, and the [most densely populated large country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population_density) in the world, with a headline population density of 1,265 people/km<sup>2</sup> as of 2020.[^cite_note-261] Bangladesh's [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) (TFR), once among the highest in the world, has experienced a dramatic decline, from 5.5 in 1985 to 3.7 in 1995, down to 1.9 in 2022,[^cite_note-262] which is below the [sub-replacement fertility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-replacement_fertility) of 2.1.[^cite_note-263] Most of the population lives in rural areas, with only 40% of the population living in urban areas as of 2023.[^cite_note-264] Bangladesh has a [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_age) of roughly 28 years, with 26% of the total population aged 14 or younger,[^cite_note-265] and 6% aged 65 and above as of 2023.[^cite_note-266]

Bangladesh is an [ethnically and culturally homogeneous society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_ranked_by_ethnic_and_cultural_diversity_level), as [Bengalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_people) form 99% of the population.[^cite_note-CIA-2021-8] The [Adivasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adivasi) population includes the [Chakmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakma_people), [Marmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marma_people), [Santhals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santhal_people), [Mros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mru_people_\(Mrucha\)), [Tanchangyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanchangya_people), [Bawms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bawm_people), [Tripuris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripuri_people), [Khasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khasi_people), [Khumis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumi_people), [Kukis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuki_people), [Garos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garo_people), and [Bisnupriya Manipuris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisnupriya_Manipuri_people). The Chittagong Hill Tracts region experienced unrest and an [insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chittagong_Hill_Tracts_conflict) from 1975 to 1997 in an autonomy movement by its indigenous people. Although a peace accord was signed in 1997, the region [remains militarised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Uttaran).[^cite_note-rashiduzzaman-267] [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu)-speaking [stranded Pakistanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stranded_Pakistanis) were given citizenship by the Supreme Court in 2008.[^cite_note-268] Bangladesh also hosts over 700,000 Rohingya refugees since 2017, giving it one of the largest refugee populations in the world.[^cite_note-RohingyaHRW-155]

### Urban centres

Further information: [List of cities and towns in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Bangladesh)

Dhaka is Bangladesh's capital and largest city. It is administered by two city corporations covering the northern and southern parts of the city. There are 13 [city corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_City_Corporations_of_Bangladesh) whose mayors are elected for five-year terms: Dhaka South, Dhaka North, [Chittagong](380451.md), [Comilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comilla), [Khulna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khulna), [Mymensingh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mymensingh), [Sylhet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylhet), [Rajshahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajshahi), [Barisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barisal), [Rangpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangpur,_Bangladesh), [Gazipur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazipur,_Dhaka_Division), [Bogura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogura) and [Narayanganj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narayanganj). The 2022 census recorded 506 urban centres, including 43 cities with populations above 100,000.[^cite_note-BBS_urban_report-269]&#x20;
